## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 1:**

The scoring rule is clear:
- **Before switch time (~400-600 steps):** Score increases for every stationary robot → robots should MOVE
- **After switch time:** Score increases for every moving robot → robots should STOP
- **Signal:** A green wall block lights up at switch time

The champion (score 10095.61) is simply `--s0 1` (Stop always). This is surprisingly good, suggesting the penalty for moving after the switch dominates, OR the robots are already somewhat stationary before the switch.

Wait - let me re-read: "Before switch time, score increases by 1 for every STATIONARY robot" and "After switch time, score increases by 1 for every MOVING robot."

So optimal behavior: **Move before switch, Stop after switch.** The green wall is the signal to stop.

The champion just Stops always, scoring ~10095. The switch happens between 400-600 steps, so on average 500 steps. Before switch: 0 penalty per step (moving robots). After switch: 700 steps × 20 robots = 14000 penalty for always moving. But always stopping gives: 500 × 20 = 10000 before + 0 after = 10000 (roughly matches 10095).

So the ideal FSM: **Explore (move) until green is detected, then Stop.**

The key perception is C=7 (perceive green color, color index 2 = green) to trigger transition from movement to stopping.

Let me build machines around this:
- State 0: Exploration (move) → transition to Stop when green wall perceived (C=7, color 2)
- State 1: Stop

**Machine designs:**

1. **Survival:** Copy champion (Stop always)
2. **Ideal 2-state:** Explore → Stop on green detection
3. **Variations:** Different exploration behaviors, multiple transitions including green detection
4. **GoToColor green:** Move toward green when detected (gets closer to wall, reinforces stopping)

- s2=GoToColor green → s1=Stop when green seen (same as s0 basically)

Actually the 3-state machine should use s2 as GoToColor as an intermediate before stopping. But the logic is complex. Let me just keep the 2-state machines mostly and have a

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 2:**

The champion (8784.54) is: Explore (rwm=50) with two transitions:
1. Stop when green detected (C=7, color=2, p=0.95)
2. Stop with tiny probability (C=5, p=0.002) — acts as a slow drift to stop even without green

The Stop state has a tiny probability (0.001) of returning to Explore — this is essentially irreversible stopping.

The core structure that works: **Move → Stop on green detection**

Key parameters to tune:
- `rwm` (turning rate during exploration): champion uses 50, top machines use 30-60
- Green detection probability: champion uses 0.95, others use 0.85-1.0
- Small random-stop probability: champion uses 0.002

**What's working well:**
- 2-state FSMs with Explore → Stop on green (C=7, l=2)
- Having a small random fallback transition (C=5, p=0.001-0.002) in explore state
- Low return probability from Stop (0.001)

**What to explore:**
- Fine-tune rwm around 40-60
- Try p=0.99 or 1.0 for green detection (deterministic)
- Try higher random-stop probability as fallback (if green never seen)
- Try 3-state with GoToColor green as intermediate state before stopping
- Try adding neighbor-based transitions

**Strategy:**
1. Survive: copy champion
2. Exploit (6-7 machines): vary rwm (35, 45, 55, 65), vary green-detection prob (0.95-1.0), vary random fallback (0.001-0.005)
3. Explore (5-6 machines): 3-state designs, different topologies, try GoToColor-green as approach state

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 3:**

The trend is clear: lower `rwm` values are better (rwm=35 wins over 40, 45, 50, 55, 60). The champion uses rwm=35. This suggests robots should explore with less random turning (more straight-line movement), which helps them cover the arena and spot the green wall faster.

The 2-state structure consistently outperforms 3-state and 4-state machines. The core pattern is rock-solid:
- State 0: Explore (rwm~30-35) with two transitions: stop on green (C=7, l=2, p=0.95) and tiny random stop (C=5, p=0.002)
- State 1: Stop with tiny random restart (C=5, p=0.001)

**What to try next:**
1. **Exploit**: Try rwm values below 35 (5, 10, 15, 20, 25, 30) — lower turning rate means straighter paths, potentially faster green detection
2. **Exploit**: Fine-tune p0x0 (green detection probability) around 0.90-0.95
3. **Exploit**: Fine-tune p0x1 (random stop fallback) around 0.001-0.003
4. **Explore**: Try rwm=1 (almost straight line), different fallback probabilities
5. **Explore**: Try adding a neighbor-based transition as additional signal

The trend strongly suggests rwm should go lower. Let me try rwm values of 5, 10, 15, 20, 25, 30 alongside the champion.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 4:**

The champion (rwm=1, score 6399.31) is dramatically better than rwm=5 (6990.62) — a gap of ~600 points. The trend is monotonically clear: **lower rwm = better**. rwm=1 is already the minimum, so we've hit the floor on that parameter.

Now we need to tune the other parameters around rwm=1:
- `p0x0` (green detection prob): champion uses 0.95. Try 0.90, 0.85, 0.99, 1.0
- `p0x1` (random stop fallback): champion uses 0.002. Try 0.001, 0.0005, 0.003, 0.005, 0.01
- `p1x0` (restart prob from stop): champion uses 0.001. Try 0.0005, 0.002, 0.0
- Also: can we eliminate the random fallback (p0x1) entirely and just rely on green detection?
- Can we add a third transition from the explore state that helps?

The random fallback (C=5, p=0.002) serves as insurance when robots never see green. But with rwm=1, robots move almost straight, covering the arena efficiently. The fallback causes some robots to stop before seeing green (wasting the "move before switch" phase slightly, but avoiding penalty after switch if green isn't seen).

**Key insight**: The random stop with p=0.002 means ~50% chance of stopping within 345 steps even without green detection. With 1200 steps total and switch at ~500, robots that stop early lose some pre-switch benefit. Maybe lower p0x1 is better now that rwm=1 covers arena more efficiently.

Also try: removing the return transition from Stop (p1x0=0 effectively — can't set 0, so set very small like 0.0001).

**Exploration ideas:**
- Try 3-state with rwm=1 and GoToColor-green as approach before stopping
- Try adding neighbor condition as additional signal in explore state
- Try different green detection probs (0.8, 0.85, 0.9, 0.95, 0.99, 1.0)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 5:**

The champion: rwm=1, p0x0=0.95 (green detection), p0x1=0.002 (random fallback), p1x0=0.0005 (restart from stop). Score: 6050.48.

The critical new insight: **p1x0=0.0005 is better than 0.001**. The return-from-stop probability should be as low as possible. This makes sense: once stopped, robots should stay stopped.

Also: p0x1=0.002 is the sweet spot for the random fallback (0.001 and 0.003 are worse). p0x0=0.95 seems good but 0.99 and 0.90 are also competitive.

**Parameter tuning priorities:**
1. `p1x0` (restart from stop): champion uses 0.0005. Try even lower: 0.0001, 0.0002. Try 0.0 effectively.
2. `p0x1` (random stop fallback): sweet spot at 0.002. Try 0.0015, 0.0018, 0.0022, 0.0025
3. `p0x0` (green detection): 0.95 wins. Nearby values 0.92, 0.97 worth trying
4. Can we eliminate p1x0 entirely or make it negligible?

The fact that p1x0=0.0005 beats 0.001 suggests we should try to minimize restart probability from stop. The floor here is that we can't set it to 0 (would need n1=0 which violates rules — every state must have transitions). So minimum is some very small value like 0.0001.

**Exploration:**
- Try 3-state with rwm=1 where unreachable state 2 is avoided — actually try having a dedicated "confirmed stop" state
- Try adding a neighbor condition as additional trigger
- Try varying the structure slightly

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 6:**

The champion: p1x0=0.0001 (1e-04), score 5518.57. The trend is crystal clear: **lower p1x0 = better**. The ranking goes perfectly: 0.0001 > 0.0002 > 0.0003 > 0.0005. We should push p1x0 even lower.

The minimum representable positive value I can use is something like 1e-05 or even 1e-06. The parameter is a probability in [0,1], so any tiny positive value should work.

Other parameters seem stable:
- p0x0 = 0.95 is fine (0.97, 0.99 slightly worse with p1x0=0.0005)
- p0x1 = 0.002 is the sweet spot (0.0018 and 0.0022 are slightly worse)

The 3-state machine with neighbor condition (rank 4) performed reasonably — worth keeping a variant.

**What to tune this round:**
1. p1x0: try 1e-05, 2e-05, 5e-05, 1e-04 (champion), 2e-04
2. p0x1: fine-tune around 0.002 with new p1x0=1e-05
3. p0x0: try 0.95, 0.97, 0.99 with new best p1x0
4. Keep 2 exploration machines with different structures

**Key question**: Can we set p1x0 effectively to 0? No — must have valid transition. But 1e-05 is practically zero (expects 100,000 steps to trigger, run is only 1200 steps).

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 7:**

The champion used p1x0=0 — the simulator accepted it! This makes the Stop state completely absorbing (no restart). Score: 5045.17.

The ranking confirms: p1x0=0 > 0.0001 > 0.0002 (monotonic). The stop state should be completely irreversible.

Now the remaining parameters to tune:
- `p0x0` (green detection): 0.95 in champion. 0.97, 0.99 are slightly worse (5360-5362 vs 5045). But this is with p1x0=0.0001 not p1x0=0. Need to test higher values with p1x0=0.
- `p0x1` (random fallback stop): 0.002 in champion. 0.0018 (rank 2 with p1x0=0.0001) is interesting. Need to test values around 0.002 with p1x0=0.
- The 3-state with neighbor condition (rank 3) also looks promising.

**Strategy for Round 8:**
1. Survive: copy champion (p1x0=0, p0x0=0.95, p0x1=0.002)
2. Exploit: vary p0x1 around 0.002 (0.0015, 0.0018, 0.002, 0.0022, 0.0025) with p1x0=0
3. Exploit: vary p0x0 (0.90, 0.92, 0.95, 0.97, 0.99, 1.0) with p1x0=0 and p0x1=0.002
4. Explore: 3-state with neighbor condition and p1x0=0
5. Explore: what if we reduce p0x1 further (0.001, 0.0005) with p1x0=0?

The random fallback (p0x1) represents robots that stop without seeing green. Too low = robots that never see green keep moving after switch (bad). Too high = robots stop too early before switch (bad). p0x1=0.002 means ~50% stop within 345 steps, which seems well-calibrated for switch at 400-600.

Actually with p1x0=0, once stopped they never restart. So p0x1 should be calibrated so most robots stop near the switch time. Switch is at 400-600 (mean 500). With p0x1=0.002, expected steps to random-stop = 500. That's exactly the mean switch time! This is a beautifully calibrated coincidence.

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 8:**

The champion still holds at 5045.17 from Round 7. This round's best was 5170 (p0x0=0.90), which didn't beat the champion. The scores are very close together (5170-5374 for ranks 1-11), suggesting we're near a local optimum.

**What the data shows:**
- p0x0: 0.90 is best this round, but 0.95 was champion last round. The difference is noise — all values 0.90-1.0 score similarly (~5170-5200). The champion's 0.95 is fine.
- p0x1: 0.002 is consistently near the top. Values below (0.001, 0.0015) and above (0.0022, 0.0025, 0.003) are worse. Sweet spot confirmed at 0.002.
- p1x0=0 is confirmed optimal.
- The 3-state GoToColor machine scored very poorly (7523) — avoid that topology.
- The 3-state with neighbor condition (rank 2 this round, 5173) is interesting!

**Key insight**: The 3-state machine with neighbor condition (n0x2: C=3, p=8, w=10) scored 5173, very close to the champion. This adds "stop if many neighbors nearby" as an additional trigger. This might help because robots cluster and a neighbor-count condition could approximate timing.

**What to try:**
1. Survive: copy champion
2. Exploit: vary p0x0 around 0.85-0.95 (maybe 0.85, 0.88, 0.90, 0.92, 0.95) with p1x0=0
3. Exploit: vary p0x1 around 0.002 (0.0019, 0.002, 0.0021) — very fine-grained
4. Exploit: 3-state with neighbor condition, vary neighbor threshold and steepness
5. Explore: try different neighbor thresholds in the 3-state machine
6. Explore: what if p0x1 is tuned differently (e.g., exponential timing to match switch distribution better)

The scores are converging. Let me focus on fine-tuning and some structural exploration.

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 9:**

The champion still holds at 5045.17. This round's top machines scored 5190-5205 — none beat the champion. 

**Important pattern**: The 3-state machines with neighbor-above condition (C=3) at the top this round all scored ~5190-5205, which is close but doesn't beat the champion 5045.17. However, C=3 with p=10, w=8 (rank 1: 5190) and p=8, w=10 (rank 3: 5205) are consistently good.

**Critical observation**: The 2-state machines (ranks 4-8, scores 5439-5443) are all essentially identical in score — p0x0 doesn't matter much (0.85-0.95 all score ~5443). This suggests p0x0 is not a key differentiator at this point.

**What differentiates good from champion**: The champion (5045) was run in Round 7, whereas the same machine in Round 8 scored 5198 and Round 9 scored 5443. This is **stochastic variance** — the champion's score of 5045 may have been a lucky run. The true expected score is probably around 5200-5440.

**Strategy for final round**: 
- Keep the champion (required)
- Focus on the 3-state with neighbor condition variants (they seem to help)
- Try different neighbor thresholds: p=10 w=8, p=8 w=10, p=9 w=9, p=7 w=12, p=6 w=15
- Try combining neighbor condition with different green detection probs
- Try 4-state machines for exploration
- Avoid C=4 (below threshold) as it scored terribly
- Fine-tune the neighbor-above condition parameters

The 3-state approach with C=3 (neighbors above threshold) seems to add value because: when robots cluster (they do with rwm=1, moving mostly straight), many neighbors being present might indicate they've all converged somewhere (possibly near the green wall). This is a useful secondary signal.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
